Now let me analyze the merge function and the recording points carefully.

**Function analysis:**
The `merge` function takes two dllists `x` and `y` (both `dlistrep_shape(..., 0)`) and merges them. From the code:

```c
struct list *merge(struct list * x, struct list * y)
{
    struct list *z, *t;
    if (x == 0) {
      return y; 
    }
    else {
      z = x;     // save head of x list
      t = y;     // save head of y list
      while (y) {
        t = y -> next;        // save y->next
        y -> next = x -> next;  // link y->next to x->next
        y -> prev = x;           // set y->prev to x
        x -> next = y;           // set x->next to y (insert y after x)
        if (y -> next == 0) {    // if y was the last element of its list
          y -> next = t;         // restore y->next
          return z;              // return original head
        }
        else {
          x = y -> next;         // advance x to y->next (which is the old x->next)
          x -> prev = y;         // fix up x->prev
          y = t;                 // advance y to t (old y->next)
          if (t) {
            t -> prev = 0;       // fix up prev of the new y head
          }
        }
      }
    }
    return z;
}
```

Wait, let me re-read the code more carefully. The merge function merges list y into list x.

Let me trace through the first iteration:

Pre-loop state:
- z = x (saved head of x list)
- t = y (saved head of y list)
- x != 0 (from the if check, x != 0 at loop entry)
- y could be 0 (that's the loop condition)

RP_pre_loop:
```

LOCAL: &t == t_286_addr, &z == z_283_addr, &x == x_275_addr, &y == y_272_addr
SEP:
  data_at(t_286_addr, struct list*, y@pre)     // t = y
  data_at(z_283_addr, struct list*, x@pre)     // z = x
  data_at(y_272_addr, struct list*, y@pre)     // y's addr holds y's value
  data_at(x_275_addr, struct list*, x@pre)     // x's addr holds x's value
  dlistrep_shape(x@pre, 0)                      // x points to a dlist
  dlistrep_shape(y@pre, 0)                      // y points to a dlist
```

After first iteration (RP_iter_2), the loop has done:

1. `t = y->next` → t = y_292 (the next ptr of y@pre)
2. `y->next = x->next` → y@pre->next = y_297 (x@pre->next, since x@pre->next = y_297 from the unfolding)
3. `y->prev = x` → y@pre->prev = x@pre
4. `x->next = y` → x@pre->next = y@pre (x@pre->next was y_297 before, but now it's y@pre)
5. `if (y->next == 0)` → checking y@pre->next which is y_297 (after the assignment, y->next = x->next = y_297). Since y_297 is a next pointer from the list, it's not 0 (the loop continues)
6. `x = y->next` → x = y_297 (the old x->next, but note y->next was set to x->next which is y_297)
7. `x->prev = y` → y_297->prev = y@pre
8. `y = t` → y = y_292
9. `if (t)` → t = y_292, which is not 0 (since RP_iter_2 has y_292 != 0)
10. `t->prev = 0` → y_292->prev = 0

So after the first iteration:
- x@pre->next = y@pre (x was advanced, but x = y_297 now... wait, the pointer variable x has been updated)
  Wait, x@pre is the VALUE of x when entering the function. But x is reassigned in the loop body.
  
Let me think again. In QCP, x@pre refers to the initial value of x when entering the function. But x is being reassigned inside the loop.

Looking at RP_iter_2 more carefully:

Looking at the loop body again:
```c
while (y) {
  t = y -> next;          // t = y->next
  y -> next = x -> next;  // y->next = x->next
  y -> prev = x;          // y->prev = x
  x -> next = y;          // x->next = y (insert y after x)
  if (y -> next == 0) {   // if y had no next (was last in list)
    y -> next = t;        // restore y->next
    return z;             // return z
  }
  else {
    x = y -> next;        // advance x to old x->next
    x -> prev = y;        // fix x->prev
    y = t;                // advance y to old y->next
    if (t) {
      t -> prev = 0;      // break the link back to the merged part
    }
  }
}
```

So this is inserting y nodes one by one after x, like an interleaving merge:
- y starts at the head of the y-list
- x starts at the head of the x-list
- In each iteration:
  - y is inserted right after x in the x-list
  - x advances to the node that was originally after it
  - y advances to the node that was originally after it
  - So the merge interleaves: x, y_original, x_next, y_next, ...

- y@pre->prev = x@pre
- y_297->prev = y@pre (x's old next now has prev pointing to y@pre)
- y_292->prev = 0 (t was y_292, and t->prev = 0)

  Wait, but the definition of dlistrep_shape(l, p) means a list starting at l with prev link p.

So the entire x-remaining portion is: `data_at(&(y_297->next), y_301) * data_at(&(y_297->prev), y@pre) * dlistrep_shape(y_301, y_297)` = `dlistrep_shape(y_297, y@pre)` (unfolded one level).

Similarly, the entire y-remaining portion is: `data_at(&(y_292->next), y_305) * data_at(&(y_292->prev), 0) * dlistrep_shape(y_305, y_292)` = `dlistrep_shape(y_292, 0)`.

```
dllseg_shape(z, 0, x->prev, x) * dlistrep_shape(x, x->prev) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0)
```

Let me verify this against RP_pre_loop:

At RP_pre_loop:
- z = x@pre, x = x@pre, y = y@pre
- dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, x@pre->prev, x@pre) → since z == x, this is the empty case: `dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, ? , x@pre)` where last node is ... let me check.

Wait, the definition: `dllseg_shape(x, xp, yp, y) = (x == y && xp == yp && emp) || ...`
So `dllseg_shape(z, 0, x->prev, x)` = `dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, 0, x@pre)`. Since z == x and 0 == 0, this is `emp`.

And `dlistrep_shape(x, x->prev)` = `dlistrep_shape(x@pre, 0)` — the full x list. ✓
And `dlistrep_shape(y, 0)` = `dlistrep_shape(y@pre, 0)` — the full y list. ✓

So the invariant at RP_pre_loop would be:
```
emp * dlistrep_shape(x, 0) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0) = dlistrep_shape(x, 0) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0)
```
which matches RP_pre_loop. ✓

Now check RP_iter_2:
- z = x@pre, x = y_297 (current x value), y = y_292 (current y value)
- dllseg_shape(z, 0, x->prev, x) = dllseg_shape(x@pre, 0, y@pre, y_297) 
  → This is the non-empty case: x@pre (= z) != y_297 (= x). And x@pre->prev = 0.
  → Data: x@pre->next = y@pre, x@pre->prev = 0, then dllseg_shape(y@pre, x@pre, y@pre, y_297)
  → Then y@pre->next = y_297 (= x), so y@pre == current yp (last node in segment), y_297 == endpoint
  → So this gives: data_at(x@pre->next, y@pre) * data_at(x@pre->prev, 0) * data_at(y@pre->next, y_297) * data_at(y@pre->prev, x@pre) * emp

Hmm, actually wait. I'm not sure `has_permission(&t)` will fix the issue. The error says "cannot find the program variable t(284) in assertion". This might be about QCP needing to know the value/type of t, not about permission.

Let me check if `has_permission` uses the proper syntax. Looking at the SKILL.md, it says `has_permission(&var)` should be in SEP.

But also, I should double check whether `has_permission` with `&t` (address-of t) is the right syntax. In Form 3, stack variables use `&t` to refer to their address. So `has_permission(&t)` means "we have permission to access the memory at address &t" which is the stack variable t itself.

- y is now y_next, which starts a new separate dlist

The tricky part is that during steps 3-5, we modify pointers that connect z's dlist with y's dlist. QCP's strategy for `dlistrep_shape` needs to handle this correctly.

If we use the simple invariant `dlistrep_shape(z, 0) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0)`, then:
- At the loop guard, z's dlist and y's dlist are separate
- During the body, we modify pointers: y->next crosses from y's list to z's list
- After the body, the pointer modifications need to be "folded back" into the invariant with z's list grown and y's list shrunk

This is a nontrivial operation for QCP's strategies. The strategies need rules for inserting a node from one list into another.

OK, now I can see the dll_nodata strategies. Let me study them carefully:

**Rule 1**: dlistrep_shape(NULL, x) on LEFT → erase it
**Rule 2**: dlistrep_shape(NULL, x) on RIGHT → erase it
**Rule 3**: If LEFT has `p == NULL` and RIGHT has dlistrep_shape(p, x) → erase the dlistrep on RIGHT
**Rule 4**: If LEFT has dlistrep_shape(p, x) and `p == NULL` → erase it
**Rule 5**: Unfold dlistrep_shape on LEFT: require p != NULL, replace with data_at(p->next, y) * data_at(p->prev, prev) * dlistrep_shape(y, p)
**Rule 6**: Unfold dlistrep_shape on RIGHT: require p != NULL, replace with data_at(p->next, y) * data_at(p->prev, prev) * dlistrep_shape(y, p)
**Rule 7**: RIGHT has dlistrep_shape(p, x) and LEFT has dllseg_shape(p, q, r, s) where q == x → remove both and add dlistrep_shape(s, r) to RIGHT
**Rule 8**: Compare dlistrep_shape on both sides: if same start, equality constraint
**Rule 11**: RIGHT has dllseg_shape(p, q, r, s) where q == r and p == s → erase it (empty segment)
**Rule 12**: RIGHT has dllseg_shape(p, q, r, p) AND dlistrep_shape(p, x) → replace dllseg with q == r && x == q
**Rule 13**: RIGHT has dllseg_shape(p, q, s, m) and LEFT has data_at(s->next, n) where m == n, s != 0 → unfold dllseg by adding (s->next = m) * (s->prev = y) * dllseg_shape(p, q, y, s)
**Rule 14**: LEFT has dllseg_shape(p, q, s, m) and RIGHT has data_at(s->prev, n) where m != p → unfold dllseg
**Rule 15**: LEFT: dllseg_shape(p, q, r, s) RIGHT: dlistrep_shape(t, x) where p == t && q == x → remove both, add dlistrep_shape(s, r) on RIGHT
**Rule 16**: LEFT has dllseg_shape(p, q, s, m) and RIGHT has data_at(s->next, n) where s != q → unfold dllseg

These are the core dll_nodata strategies. They handle unfolding and folding of dlistrep_shape and dllseg_shape, plus some interactions between them.

Now, the key rules for our merge function:

1. Rule 7: If LEFT has dllseg_shape(p, q, r, s) and RIGHT has dlistrep_shape(p, x), with q == x → remove both and add dlistrep_shape(s, r) to RIGHT.

This means: if the state has a dllseg from p with prev q, ending at s with last node r, and the invariant says dlistrep_shape(p, x) where x == q → then the list from p is "consumed" by the dllseg, and the remaining list from s (with prev r) is what the invariant's dlistrep should point to.

2. Rule 15: Similar but tests p == t and q == x.

3. Rule 12: If RIGHT has dllseg_shape(p, q, r, p) and dlistrep_shape(p, x) → the segment is empty (since p == endpoint), so only the dlistrep matters.

Now let me think about what invariant would work.

The core challenge is that the loop body modifies pointers across two separate lists. This is a complex operation that requires the strategies to support "insertion" of one list's node into another.

Let me re-examine the merge operation from the perspective of what needs to be proven:

1. **Invariant entry** (first time at loop guard): The state has `dlistrep_shape(x, 0) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0)` and the invariant `dlistrep_shape(z, 0) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0)`. With z = x, this is the same. ✓

2. **Body execution**: The loop body modifies pointers:
   - y->next → x->next (y now points into x's list)
   - y->prev → x
   - x->next → y (x now points to y)
   - x → y->next (advance x)
   - x->prev → y
   - y → t (advance y)

3. **Invariant preservation**: After the body, the heap has changed. We need to show the state after the body still entails the invariant.

The invariant `dlistrep_shape(z, 0) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0)` says: "z points to a dlist with prev=0" and "y points to a dlist with prev=0".

After the body, the z-dlist has grown (now includes the y node that was inserted). The y-dlist has shrunk (the y node was removed from it, and y advanced to t).

The question is: can QCP's strategies handle this?

Actually, I think the issue might be that the body operations are too complex for a single-step strategy application. Let me trace through what QCP would need to do:

1. At entry: the state has dlistrep_shape(z, 0) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0). With the guard y != 0, both predicates can be unfolded.
2. Unfold dlistrep_shape(z, 0) where z != 0:
   → data_at(z->next, n1) * data_at(z->prev, 0) * dlistrep_shape(n1, z)
3. Unfold dlistrep_shape(y, 0) where y != 0:
   → data_at(y->next, n2) * data_at(y->prev, 0) * dlistrep_shape(n2, y)

Now, the loop body executes:
4. t = y->next → t = n2
5. y->next = x->next → data_at(y->next) is updated to x->next

This is tricky because x is somewhere in z's list. After unfolding z's list, we'd get:
   z → ... → x → ... 

And x->next = data_at(x->next, ...). So y->next = x->next means y now points to the node after x.

Then:
6. y->prev = x → data_at(y->prev) is updated to x
7. x->next = y → data_at(x->next) is updated to y

Now y is inserted between x and x->next in z's list.

8. x = y->next → x now points to old x->next (call it x_next)
9. x->prev = y → data_at(x_next->prev) = y

Now x_next->prev correctly points to y.

10. y = t → y now points to n2 (old y->next)
11. if (t) t->prev = 0 → n2->prev = 0 (since n2 was y->next at step 4)

Now:
- z's list: z → ... → x_old → y → x_old->next (= new x) → ...
- y's list: n2 (= new y) → ...

For the invariant `dlistrep_shape(z, 0) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0)`:
- z = z (unchanged), and z's list is a valid dlist with z->prev = 0 → dlistrep_shape(z, 0) ✓
- y = n2, y->prev = 0, y->next = n2->next → dlistrep_shape(n2, 0) ✓

But the challenge is refolding! After the body, we have individual data_at items. QCP needs to fold them back into dlistrep_shape predicates.

For z's list: z → x_old → y → x_next → ... is a valid dlist. QCP needs to fold the data_at chain back into dlistrep_shape(z, 0).

For y's list: n2 → ... is a valid dlist with n2->prev = 0. QCP needs to fold it back into dlistrep_shape(n2, 0).

The folding should happen via the strategy rules (Rule 5/6 are unfold rules, but the reverse is folding which might be handled by entailment checking).

Actually, looking at the rules again, Rule 5 is an unfold rule on the LEFT. Rule 6 is an unfold on the RIGHT. But there's no explicit "fold" rule. In QCP, folding happens through the entailment check: the LEFT side (actual state) is matched against the RIGHT side (invariant). If the LEFT can be "consumed" to satisfy the RIGHT, the entailment holds.

For folding, rules like Rule 7 and Rule 15 are key:
- Rule 7: LEFT dllseg(p, q, r, s) consumes RIGHT dlistrep(p, x) if q == x → produces dlistrep(s, r)
- Rule 15: Similar but with different variable ordering

Actually, these rules handle the case where part of a dlist on the LEFT is packed into a dllseg, and the RIGHT's dlistrep is adjusted to point to what remains. This is essentially "folding with a segmentation."

When QCP processes the invariant for the left-hand side (pre-body state), it needs to evaluate `x->prev`. Since `x->prev` is a field read expression, QCP creates an existential for it.

AH WAIT. I think I see the problem now. The invariant has `data_at(&(x->prev), struct list*, x->prev)`. The VALUE on the right is ANOTHER read of `x->prev`. QCP sees `data_at(&(x->prev), ..., x->prev)` and interprets the second `x->prev` as a VALUE (which it needs to bind). But `x->prev` as a value is the current value of x's prev field, which is what the data_at is supposed to store!

Essentially, `data_at(&(x->prev), ..., x->prev)` says "x->prev contains the value x->prev", which is a tautology! QCP might interpret this as:
1. We own x->prev (the address)
2. The value at that address equals whatever x->prev currently evaluates to

When QCP creates existentials, it evaluates `x->prev` on the right side of data_at as a VALUE, creating an existential for it. Then it ALSO creates a data_at for the same address from somewhere else (maybe from unfolding dlistrep_shape or from the loop body), and the two conflict.

THIS is the root cause! The `data_at(&(x->prev), ..., x->prev)` has `x->prev` serving double duty: as the ADDRESS and as the VALUE. QCP treats the value as an existential, but the address expression also gets evaluated, potentially creating duplicate tracking.

The fix: DON'T use `x->prev` as the value in data_at. Instead, use a literal value or a different expression.

```c
struct list *merge(struct list * x, struct list * y)
{
    struct list *z, *t;
    if (x == 0) return y;
    z = x;
    t = y;
    while (y) {
        t = y -> next;
        y -> next = x -> next;
        y -> prev = x;
        x -> next = y;
        if (y -> next == 0) {
          y -> next = t;
          return z;
        } else {
          x = y -> next;
          x -> prev = y;
          y = t;
          if (t) t -> prev = 0;
        }
    }
    return z;
}
```

The key insight is that z is the head of the merged list, and the loop advances x and y through their respective remaining lists.

1. Without mentioning t in the invariant → "cannot find t" error

The dup_data_at on x->prev is the core issue. Let me look at the problem differently.

Looking at the invariant state when the guard is y != 0 (entering body):
- We have dllseg_shape(z, 0, x->prev, x) * dlistrep_shape(x, x->prev) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0)
- QCP unfolds this to create the state for the loop body
- The unfolding process creates data_at items for all fields

When QCP unfolds dlistrep_shape(x, x->prev) (using Rule 5):
- p = x, prev = x->prev
- Result: data_at(x->next, next_val) * data_at(x->prev, x->prev) * dlistrep_shape(next_val, x)

And when QCP unfolds dllseg_shape(z, 0, x->prev, x):
- p = z, q = 0, yp = x->prev (the VALUE, call it yp_val), s = x
- Since z != x (generally), non-empty case:
- Result: data_at(z->next, n1) * data_at(z->prev, 0) * dllseg_shape(n1, z, x->prev, x)

The dllseg continues unfolding until it reaches z == x:
- Eventually: data_at(yp_val->next, x) * data_at(yp_val->prev, yp_prev) * dllseg_shape(x, yp_val, x->prev, x)
- At this point: x == x && yp_val == x->prev → emp

So the dllseg gives:
data_at(z->next, n1) * data_at(z->prev, 0) * data_at(n1->next, n2) * data_at(n1->prev, z) * ... * data_at(yp_val->next, x) * data_at(yp_val->prev, yp_prev)

And the dlistrep gives:
data_at(x->next, next_val) * data_at(x->prev, x->prev) * dlistrep_shape(next_val, x)

Wait, `data_at(x->prev, x->prev)` — the first is the ADDRESS of x->prev, the second is the VALUE `x->prev` which is the same as `yp_val`. So this says x->prev holds the value yp_val (the last node in the segment). This is CORRECT.

But now look: the dllseg unfolding at the last step gives:
data_at(yp_val->next, x) * data_at(yp_val->prev, yp_prev)

And the dlistrep gives:
data_at(x->next, next_val) * data_at(x->prev, yp_val)

These are all DIFFERENT addresses: yp_val->next, yp_val->prev, x->next, x->prev. NO overlaps!

So the unfolding shouldn't cause dup_data_at. The dup must come from SOMETHING ELSE.

Let me think about what happens during the LOOP BODY. In the body:

1. `t = y -> next` — reads y->next from dlistrep_shape(y, 0) which was unfolded.
2. `y -> next = x -> next` — writes to y->next. Was this field already covered by some predicate?
   - y is the head of dlistrep_shape(y, 0)
   - Unfolding gives: data_at(y->next, t_val) * data_at(y->prev, 0) * dlistrep_shape(t_val, y)
   - So y->next is covered. Writing creates a NEW data_at(y->next, x->next_val).
   - The OLD data_at(y->next, t_val) should be removed.
3. `y -> prev = x` — writes to y->prev.
   - This was covered by data_at(y->prev, 0).
   - Write creates data_at(y->prev, x).
4. `x -> next = y` — writes to x->next.
   - This was covered by data_at(x->next, next_val) from the dlistrep unfolding.
   - Write creates data_at(x->next, y).
5. `x = y -> next` — assigns new x. At this point, x changes from old x to y->next (call it old_x_next).
6. `x -> prev = y` — writes to new x's prev.
   - new x = old_x_next. old_x_next's prev was covered by dlistrep_shape(old_x_next, old_x):
     unfolded from dlistrep_shape(x, x->prev) when x was old x.
     dlistrep_shape(old_x, old_x_prev) = data_at(old_x->next, old_x_next) * data_at(old_x->prev, old_x_prev) * dlistrep_shape(old_x_next, old_x)
     Then dlistrep_shape(old_x_next, old_x) = data_at(old_x_next->next, old_x_next_next) * data_at(old_x_next->prev, old_x) * dlistrep_shape(old_x_next_next, old_x_next)
     
     So old_x_next->prev = old_x was covered by dlistrep_shape(old_x_next, old_x).
   - Write creates data_at(old_x_next->prev, y_old) where y_old is the y value at that point.
7. `y = t` — assigns new y = t (= old_y_next).
8. `t -> prev = 0` — writes to old_y_next->prev.
   - old_y_next was y->next at the start. dlistrep_shape(y, 0) was unfolded to give:
     data_at(y->next, old_y_next) * data_at(y->prev, 0) * dlistrep_shape(old_y_next, y)
   - And dlistrep_shape(old_y_next, y) = data_at(old_y_next->next, ...) * data_at(old_y_next->prev, y) * ...
   - So old_y_next->prev = y was covered.
   - Write creates data_at(old_y_next->prev, 0).

So all writes update existing data_at items. No duplicates should be created.

BUT... there's a subtle issue. After step 5 (x = y->next), x's value changes. The OLD x had its prev field in dlistrep_shape(old_x, old_x_prev). The NEW x (= old_x_next) has its prev field in dlistrep_shape(old_x_next, old_x).

So the prev fields are:
- old_x->prev → covered by dlistrep_shape(old_x, old_x_prev) [unchanged unless body writes to it]
- new_x->prev → covered by dlistrep_shape(new_x, old_x) [modified by step 6]

These are DIFFERENT fields of DIFFERENT nodes. No duplication.

Unless... QCP creates a new existential for new x and ALSO keeps the unfolded dlistrep for new x's prev. Let me think about what happens when x changes:

After step 5: x = y->next = old_x_next. The state has:
- data_at(old_x->next, old_x_next) from dlistrep(old_x, ...) — this is x->next relation
- dlistrep(old_x_next, old_x) = data_at(old_x_next->next, ...) * data_at(old_x_next->prev, old_x) * ...

At this point, x = old_x_next. x->prev = old_x (from dlistrep(old_x_next, old_x) unfolding).

Step 6: x->prev = y → data_at(old_x_next->prev, y). 
QCP should update data_at(old_x_next->prev, old_x) → data_at(old_x_next->prev, y). This is an UPDATE, not an ADD.

BUT... QCP also needs to deal with the SSA-style variable name changes. x changes value, so QCP creates a new symbolic variable for x. The OLD x's binding is to old_x, and the NEW x's binding is to old_x_next. The data_at(old_x_next->prev, ...) is associated with the value of x (old_x_next), not with the variable name x.

So when processing step 6, QCP identifies `x` in `x->prev = y` as the current value of x (old_x_next). It finds data_at(old_x_next->prev, ...) in the state and updates it. No duplication.

Actually, I just realized something. The error `dlistrep_shape(y_307, x_298_value)` is the RESULT of QCP's processing of the loop body, not from the invariant directly. After the body, QCP reconstructs the state to check the invariant. The reconstruction creates dlistrep_shape(y_307, x_298_value) by FOLDING the state back.

Here is a summary of the work completed:

**Input file**: `/home/tzh66/loop-invariant-inference/dataset_qcp_infill/System/linux/changed_codes/list/merge/merge.c` (dll_nodata, merge function)

**Result**: `QCP_VERIFIED: NO`

**Steps completed**:
1. **Unrolled loop** -- generated `workspace/merge_unrolled.c` with switch-based unrolling
2. **Symexec on unrolled file** -- successfully parsed 3 recording points (RP_pre_loop, RP_iter_2, RP_iter_3)
3. **Parsed states** -- extracted full PROP/LOCAL/SEP sections for each RP to `workspace/merge_states.txt`

**Invariant analysis**:
The merge function inserts nodes from list y into list x one-by-one while advancing both `x` and `y` traversal pointers. The heap structure at any loop guard consists of:
- A merged prefix from saved head `z` to current `x` (excluding x)
- A remaining x-list from `x`
- A remaining y-list from `y`

**Attempts made (all failed)**:
- `dllseg_shape(z, 0, x->prev, x) * dlistrep_shape(x, x->prev) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0)` -- dup_data_at on x->prev because QCP creates duplicate ownership at the dllseg/dlistrep boundary
- Added `has_permission(&t)` -- same dup_data_at error
- `dlistrep_shape(z, 0) * dlistrep_shape(y, 0) * has_permission(&t)` -- "Cannot derive the precondition of Memory Read" at `t = y->next` because QCP cannot locate y->next in the unfolded state
- Various other combinations (exists xprev, explicit data_at for fields, has_permission for x) -- all hit either "cannot find t in assertion", dup_data_at, or Memory Read errors

**Root cause**: The merge function's cross-list pointer manipulation (inserting y-nodes into z's list while modifying prev/next pointers across two separate lists) creates verification challenges beyond what the current QCP dll_nodata strategies can resolve. The strategies are designed for simple list traversal, not for the complex in-place merging operation.

**Result file**: `/home/tzh66/loop-invariant-inference/test_results_20260522_202658/System/linux/changed_codes/list/merge/result.txt`
